1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	We organize results around seven findings (F1--F7) that trace a predictive pathway from trained temporal conditioning to pathological value dynamics. All experiments use Walker Walk (DMC) with DreamerV3; treatments run to 105K steps and the baseline to 505K steps. We report evaluation returns (mean over 10 episodes) and the normalized value-to-eval ratio ($\nve$, \S\ref{sec:metrics}).
5	
6	%% ============================================================
7	%% TABLE 1: Main results summary
8	%% ============================================================
9	
10	\begin{table*}[t]
11	\centering
12	\caption{Key configurations and outcomes. NVE reports peak $\nve$ in the 35K+ window (\S\ref{sec:metrics}). CIVO-positive seeds are shaded. Complete results for all 43 configurations in Table~\ref{tab:complete_results}.}
13	\label{tab:main_results}
14	\footnotesize
15	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
16	\begin{tabular}{llcccccl}
17	\toprule
18	\textbf{Exp} & \textbf{Config} & \textbf{dt\_emb} & \textbf{Sub} & \textbf{H} & \textbf{Eval@final} & \textbf{NVE} & \textbf{Outcome} \\
19	\midrule
20	001 & Baseline ($\dt{=}1$) & --- & --- & 15 & 915.7 @105K & 1.00$\times$ & Healthy \\
21	\midrule
22	\rowcolor{gray!15}
23	008d & Factorial (s1) & trn & ON & 8 & 82.8 @55K & 4.12$\times$ & Full collapse \\
24	008f & No dt\_emb (s1) & --- & ON & 8 & 503.0 @105K & 0.97$\times$ & Healthy \\
25	008a & Unexposed emb & exp$^a$ & OFF & 15 & 777.7 @105K & 0.93$\times$ & Healthy \\
26	015b & Frozen emb & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 204.2 @105K & 1.45$\times$ & Grad.\ starvation \\
27	022b & Frozen emb + Gumbel gate & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 300.7 @105K & 0.63$\times$ & No CIVO \\
28	\midrule
29	\multicolumn{8}{l}{\textit{Cross-environment validation}} \\
30	\rowcolor{gray!15}
31	010b & Cheetah Run & trn & ON & 8 & 51.7 @105K & 8.27$\times$ & Collapsed \\
32	019b & PickCube-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 26.64 @200K & 3.78$\times$$^c$ & Transient onset \\
33	049 & StackCube-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 34.9 @212K & 5.05$\times$$^d$ & Env.\ transient \\
34	050 & PegInsertionSide-v1 & trn & ON & 8 & 20.86 @212K & 10.05$\times$$^e$ & Env.\ dynamics$^e$ \\
35	046 & Cheetah Run & frz$^b$ & ON & 8 & 150.5 @215K & 1.35$\times$ & No CIVO \\

44	$^c$ NVE normalized against exp\_019a\_v2 (vanilla DreamerV3 on PickCube-v1).\\
45	$^d$ Frozen control (exp\_051) shows identical spike ($4.30\times$), confirming environment-specific origin.\\

50	
51	%% ============================================================
52	%% F1: All dt>1 degrade
53	%% ============================================================
54	
55	\subsection{F1: All Variable-Rate Configurations Degrade}
56	\label{sec:f1}
57	
58	Every configuration that introduces $\dt > 1$ imagination degrades relative to the $\dt{=}1$ baseline (Figure~\ref{fig:learning_curves}). The baseline reaches 915.7 at 105K; fixed $\dt{=}2$ (006) peaks at 485.3 before collapsing to 132.9 ($14\%$ of baseline); fixed $\dt{=}4$ (007) never exceeds 176.9 ($19\%$); the learned gate (005) collapses to 98.8 ($11\%$).
59	
60	\begin{figure}[t]
61	\centering
62	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig1_learning_curves.pdf}
63	\caption{Learning curves. The baseline (black) reaches 915.7; all variable-rate configurations degrade.}
64	\label{fig:learning_curves}
65	\end{figure}
66	
67	%% ============================================================
68	%% F2: Trained dt_embedding → nve > 1.5x
69	%% ============================================================
70	
71	\subsection{F2: Trained Temporal Embedding Precedes Value Overestimation}
72	\label{sec:f2}
73	
74	The factorial design isolates trained $\dt$-embedding as the strongest predictor. The \emph{primary} comparison---008d (trained, subsample ON) vs.\ 008f (no embedding, subsample ON)---differs only in embedding training, yielding $\nve = 4.12\times$ (eval 82.8) vs.\ $\nve \leq 0.97\times$ (eval 503.0). \emph{Secondary} comparisons against 008a/008c (unexposed embedding) co-vary subsample status and horizon alongside the embedding factor. The $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ threshold (35K+ window) correctly classifies 27 of 30 experimental configurations across two environments (AUROC${=}0.957$, in-sample; LOEO cross-validated, Table~\ref{tab:nve_loeo}; Figure~\ref{fig:nve_trajectory}).
75	\begin{figure}[t]
76	\centering
77	\includegraphics[width=0.65\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_nve_trajectory.pdf}
78	\caption{NVE trajectories (mean $\pm$ seed range). Trained $\dt$-embedding (red, Walker Walk, $n{=}3$ seeds plotted; seeds~4 and~5 data in Table~\ref{tab:complete_results}) exceeds the $1.5\times$ threshold (dashed) in all seeds; no-embedding control (blue) remains near $1.0\times$. Three trajectory types emerge: early collapse (seed~0), late-onset collapse (seed~3), and self-correction (seed~2). Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}
79	\label{fig:nve_trajectory}
80	\end{figure}
81	
82	The trained $\dt$-embedding precedes CIVO in all five Walker Walk seeds (peak NVE $1.53$--$4.72\times$), universally degrading performance to below 30\% of baseline (IQM $148.8$, $n{=}5$; \citealt{agarwal2021precipice}). Combined with Cheetah Run (\S\ref{sec:f6}), CIVO onset is universal across all seven tested seeds ($n{=}7$, two environments) with 6/7 suffering sustained policy collapse, 1/7 self-correcting (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
83	
84	%% ============================================================
85	%% F3--F5: Summary (details in Appendix B)
86	%% ============================================================
87	
88	\subsection{F3--F5: Factorial Ablation (Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial})}
89	\label{sec:f3f5_summary}
90	
91	Trained $\dt$-embedding and multi-rate data exposure are \emph{jointly sufficient in tested configurations} for CIVO. Neither factor alone precedes pathological overestimation: trained embedding without multi-rate exposure (008a, 3 seeds, peak NVE $0.83$--$0.93\times$) and multi-rate exposure without embedding (008f, 3 seeds, peak NVE $\leq 1.41\times$) both remain below $1.5\times$. Cross-environment replication on Cheetah Run confirms generalization. Freezing the embedding (\texttt{requires\_grad=False}) dramatically reduces severity (NVE $0.19$--$1.88\times$ vs.\ trained $1.53$--$4.72\times$; 2/4 seeds below threshold, 2/4 transient self-correcting spikes; Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb}); removing the embedding (008f) also avoids CIVO. Partial gradient blocking (exp\_044) does not prevent onset (peak NVE $4.45\times$) but permits delayed self-correction (final eval 538, 59\% baseline; Appendix~\ref{sec:appendix_factorial}), confirming that \emph{any} gradient pathway updating $e_{\dt}$ suffices to induce CIVO. Degradation scales monotonically with $\dt$ ($-47\%$ at $\dt{=}2$, $-81\%$ at $\dt{=}4$; Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}).
92	
93	\paragraph{ManiSkill manipulation tasks.}
94	None of three ManiSkill environments exhibit $\dt$-embedding-dependent CIVO. All value-ratio spikes ($4$--$11\times$) are environment-specific: frozen controls show comparable or higher peaks (Table~\ref{tab:cross_domain}), and all self-correct. CIVO is locomotion-specific in DreamerV3; the frozen-embedding diagnostic correctly identifies these transients as non-CIVO.
95	
96	%% ============================================================
97	%% F6--F7: Interventions and cross-environment
98	%% ============================================================
99	
100	\subsection{F6--F7: Interventions and Multiple Pathways}
101	\label{sec:f6}
102	
103	\paragraph{F6: Trained embedding creates multiple vulnerability pathways.}
104	Exp\_008e\_v3 restricts imagination to $\dt{=}1$ while retaining the trained embedding. Two of four seeds collapse (50\%): Pathway~A (CIVO, $\nve = 4.02\times$) and preliminary Pathway~B (entropy \emph{increase}, $\nve = 3.78\times$; 2 seeds only, requires confirmation; see Figure~\ref{fig:multiple_pathways}). Pathway~B produces one of three non-CIVO false positives ($n{=}30$).
105	
106	\begin{figure}[t]
107	\centering
108	\includegraphics[width=0.75\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig6_multiple_pathways.pdf}
109	\caption{Contrasting collapse dynamics. \textbf{Left:} 008d (CIVO), value explosion with entropy decrease. \textbf{Right:} 008e\_v3 s2 (non-CIVO), evaluation crash with entropy increase.}
110	\label{fig:multiple_pathways}
111	\end{figure}
112	\paragraph{F7: Full-pathway gradient stopping shows mixed results.}
113	Full-SGS (exp\_018) detaches all non-KL gradients from $e_{\dt}$. Seed~1 shows partial mitigation (eval 448.3, NVE $1.66\times$); seed~2 collapses (0/2 elimination; Appendix~\ref{sec:sgs_gradient_analysis}). Cross-environment validation on Cheetah (010b, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\times$) confirms CIVO generalization; full-SGS yields 0/3 elimination. Removing the embedding eliminates CIVO on Cheetah (024b, NVE $< 0.2\times$).
114	
115	\paragraph{Targeted obs-decoder-only SGS confirms pathway redundancy.}
116	Obs-decoder-only SGS (exp\_025/026) achieves 1/6 CIVO prevention (1/4 Walker, 0/2 Cheetah; Table~\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}), not meaningfully different from full-pathway SGS (0/3). CIVO operates through redundant gradient channels: blocking any single pathway shifts co-adaptation to the remaining ones (Appendix~\ref{sec:seed_divergence}).
117	
118	\begin{table}[t]
119	\centering
120	\caption{Intervention hierarchy. Prevention = peak NVE $< 1.5\times$ (35K+). No level reliably eliminates CIVO across environments.}
121	\label{tab:intervention_hierarchy}
122	\footnotesize
123	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{3pt}
124	\begin{tabular}{llccc}

126	\textbf{Level} & \textbf{Intervention} & \textbf{Seeds} & \textbf{Prev.} & \textbf{Rate} \\

128	--- & Untreated & 7 & 0/7 & 0\% \\
129	\midrule
130	Gradient & Reward-only SGS & 2 & 0/2 & 0\% \\

132	Gradient & Obs-decoder SGS & 6 & 1/6 & 17\% \\

134	Output & RAVE ($c{=}1.5{,}2.0$) & 2 & 0/2 & 0\% \\
135	\midrule
136	Repr. & TCN ($\sigma{=}0.1$) & 1 & 1/1$^\ddagger$ & 100\% \\
137	Repr. & TCN ($\sigma{=}0.2$) & 6$^\dagger$ & 2/6 & 33\% \\
138	Repr. & TCN ($\sigma{=}0.5$) & 3 & 0/3 & 0\% \\

140	\midrule

151	$^\ddagger$Borderline (NVE${=}1.02\times$, eval 55\%).
152	$^\S$CIVO prevented but progressive policy erosion (peak 116\%, final 15\% baseline).
153	$^\|$Walker 3/3 ($\nve \leq 1.4\times$; 1/3 non-CIVO eval collapse), Cheetah 0/1.\\
154	$^\#$Walker 5 + Cheetah 2. Exp\_047 (NVE $1.71\times$) and Exp\_057 (NVE $1.88\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps; remaining 5 seeds: NVE $0.19$--$1.45\times$.\par}
155	\end{table}
156	
157	%% ============================================================
158	%% Forward-pass interventions
159	%% ============================================================
160	
161	\subsection{Forward-Pass Interventions}
162	\label{sec:forward_results}
163	
164	We test two forward-pass alternatives (Table~\ref{tab:intervention_hierarchy}). \textbf{RAVE} (value-level capping) fails: the EMA anchor tracks biased values during CIVO onset. \textbf{TCN} (representation-level noise) suppresses CIVO in 2 of 4 Walker seeds at $\sigma{=}0.2$ (NVE $< 1.0\times$, eval 45--91\%), but remaining seeds show runaway escalation, suggesting TCN gates rather than attenuates CIVO. Cross-environment transfer fails: Cheetah effective dose ($\sigma{=}1.0$) causes policy erosion to 15\% baseline; PickCube 0/1 (Appendix~\ref{sec:tcn_cross_env}). TCN is a \emph{diagnostic probe}, not a deployable mitigation.
165	
166	%% ============================================================
167	%% Architecture-Level Ablation
168	%% ============================================================
169	
170	\subsection{Architecture-Level Ablation}
171	\label{sec:arch_results}
172	
173	\paragraph{Critic convergence in pure imagination.}
174	Across all dual-critic configurations ($n{=}7$), critics trained on shared RSSM features converge ($\rho > 0.999$), with pessimism at 3--4\% of the independent-critic bound (\S\ref{sec:dual_critic_method}). Pessimistic estimation is thus ineffective; the min operation in wide dual-critic may provide regularization through mechanisms that remain to be characterized (Appendix~\ref{sec:dual_critic_appendix}).
175	
176	\paragraph{$2{\times}2$ factorial: recovery vs.\ prevention.}
177	Min drives recovery: narrow dual-critic (${\sim}1.2$M) recovers fully (${\sim}16$--$19\times$ recovery ratio) despite $2\times$ fewer parameters. Prevention requires min $+$ capacity: wide dual-critic maintains $\nve \leq 1.4\times$ (3/3 prevention, 1/3 non-CIVO collapse). Cross-environment transfer is limited (Appendix~\ref{sec:dual_critic_appendix}).
178	
179	%% ============================================================
180	%% Online CIVO Detection
181	%% ============================================================
182	
183	\subsection{Online CIVO Detection}
184	\label{sec:detector}
185	
186	We derive a lightweight online detector monitoring the value-to-evaluation ratio for superlinear growth (Algorithm~\ref{alg:civo_detector}). Applied to 14 runs across three environments, it achieves TPR${=}5/5$ (95\% Clopper-Pearson CI $[47.8\%, 100\%]$), FPR${=}2/9$ ($22\%$), with predictive alerts in 2/5 cases (Appendix~\ref{sec:detector_appendix}). The detector requires larger-sample validation before deployment as a practical diagnostic tool.
187	
188	\begin{algorithm}[t]
189	\caption{Online CIVO Detector. Monitors the value-to-evaluation ratio $r_t = \bar{V}_t / \bar{E}_t$ (not NVE) for superlinear growth indicative of CIVO onset.}
190	\label{alg:civo_detector}
191	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
192	\REQUIRE Stream of $(\bar{V}_t, \bar{E}_t)$ at each checkpoint $t$
193	\STATE $\tau_\text{mag} \leftarrow 0.49$, \quad $\tau_\text{vel} \leftarrow 0.1$, \quad $\varepsilon \leftarrow 10^{-6}$
194	\STATE Initialize $r_\text{prev} \leftarrow 0$, $\Delta r_\text{prev} \leftarrow 0$, $\mathcal{H} \leftarrow []$
195	\FOR{each checkpoint $t$}
196	  \STATE $r_t \leftarrow \bar{V}_t \,/\, \max(\bar{E}_t,\, \varepsilon)$
197	  \STATE $\Delta r_t \leftarrow r_t - r_\text{prev}$
198	  \STATE $c_\text{mag} \leftarrow (r_t \geq \tau_\text{mag})$
199	  \STATE $c_\text{vel} \leftarrow (\Delta r_t > \tau_\text{vel})$
200	  \STATE $c_\text{acc} \leftarrow (\Delta r_\text{prev} > \tau_\text{vel}) \wedge (\Delta r_t > \Delta r_\text{prev})$
201	  \STATE $c_\text{dec} \leftarrow \bigl|\{i : \mathcal{H}[i] < \mathcal{H}[i{-}1],\; i \in \text{last 3}\}\bigr| \geq 2$
202	  \IF{$c_\text{mag} \wedge c_\text{vel} \wedge c_\text{acc} \wedge c_\text{dec}$}
203	    \STATE \textbf{Alert:} CIVO onset at step $t$
204	  \ENDIF
205	  \STATE $r_\text{prev} \leftarrow r_t$, \quad $\Delta r_\text{prev} \leftarrow \Delta r_t$
206	  \STATE Append $\bar{E}_t$ to $\mathcal{H}$
207	\ENDFOR
208	\end{algorithmic}
209	\end{algorithm}
210	
```

</details>

Dotted line marks the 35K diagnostic window onset.}\n\\label{fig:nve_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe trained $\\dt$-embedding precedes CIVO in all five Walker Walk seeds (peak NVE $1.53$--$4.72\\times$), universally degrading performance to below 30\\% of baseline (IQM $148.8$, $n{=}5$; \\citealt{agarwal2021precipice}). Applied to 14 runs across three environments, it achieves TPR${=}5/5$ (95\\% Clopper-Pearson CI $[47.8\\%, 100\\%]$), FPR${=}2/9$ ($22\\%$), with predictive alerts in 2/5 cases (Appendix~\\ref{sec:detector_appendix}). The detector requires larger-sample validation before deployment as a practical diagnostic tool.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Online CIVO Detector. Monitors the value-to-evaluation ratio $r_t = \\bar{V}_t / \\bar{E}_t$ (not NVE) for superlinear growth indicative of CIVO onset.}\n\\label{alg:civo_detector}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\REQUIRE Stream of $(\\bar{V}_t, \\bar{E}_t)$ at each checkpoint $t$\n\\STATE $\\tau_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow 0.49$, \\quad $\\tau_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow 0.1$, \\quad $\\varepsilon \\leftarrow 10^{-6}$\n\\STATE Initialize $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow 0$, $\\mathcal{H} \\leftarrow []$\n\\FOR{each checkpoint $t$}\n  \\STATE $r_t \\leftarrow \\bar{V}_t \\,/\\, \\max(\\bar{E}_t,\\, \\varepsilon)$\n  \\STATE $\\Delta r_t \\leftarrow r_t - r_\\text{prev}$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{mag} \\leftarrow (r_t \\geq \\tau_\\text{mag})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{vel} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_t > \\tau_\\text{vel})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{acc} \\leftarrow (\\Delta r_\\text{prev} > \\tau_\\text{vel}) \\wedge (\\Delta r_t > \\Delta r_\\text{prev})$\n  \\STATE $c_\\text{dec} \\leftarrow \\bigl|\\{i : \\mathcal{H}[i] < \\mathcal{H}[i{-}1],\\; i \\in \\text{last 3}\\}\\bigr| \\geq 2$\n  \\IF{$c_\\text{mag} \\wedge c_\\text{vel} \\wedge c_\\text{acc} \\wedge c_\\text{dec}$}\n    \\STATE \\textbf{Alert:} CIVO onset at step $t$\n  \\ENDIF\n  \\STATE $r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow r_t$, \\quad $\\Delta r_\\text{prev} \\leftarrow \\Delta r_t$\n  \\STATE Append $\\bar{E}_t$ to $\\mathcal{H}$\n\\ENDFOR\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n",
      "numLines": 210,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 210
    }
  }
}

117	
118	\section{NVE Threshold Sensitivity Analysis}
119	\label{sec:appendix_sensitivity}
120	
121	We report the full sensitivity analysis for the NVE diagnostic framework introduced in Section~\ref{sec:results}. The main text reports Task~B2 (any CIVO detection, $\nve \geq 1.5\times$). Here we additionally present Task~B1, which restricts the positive class to \emph{sustained} CIVO, specifically experiments exhibiting persistent, accelerating value overestimation that progresses to performance collapse. Transient CIVO cases (e.g., exp~009b, which self-corrects after a brief excursion above the threshold) are classified as negative in B1.
122	
123	\subsection{Per-Experiment NVE Values}
124	
125	Table~\ref{tab:nve_raw} lists the NVE values at 35K+ training steps for all eleven factorial experiments (including four 008e\_v3 seeds and exp\_059). Experiments are sorted by descending NVE. Experiment 008e\_v3\_s2 exhibits elevated NVE ($3.78\times$) due to Imagination-Reality Divergence (IRD), a mechanistically distinct phenomenon from CIVO, and is classified as negative in both tasks. Experiment 008e\_v3\_s3 exhibits CIVO with NVE $4.02\times$ despite $\Delta t{=}1$ imagination.
126	
127	\begin{table}[ht]
128	\centering
129	\caption{Per-experiment NVE values (35K+ steps) and ground truth labels. $+$ = positive (CIVO), $-$ = negative. IRD = Imagination-Reality Divergence; trans.\ = transient CIVO with self-correction.}
130	\label{tab:nve_raw}
131	\small
132	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrccc@{}}
133	\toprule
134	Experiment & NVE & Mechanism & B1 & B2 \\
135	\midrule
136	006          & 4.83 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\
137	059          & 4.72 & self-correcting CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\
138	008d         & 4.12 & sustained CIVO & $+$ & $+$ \\
139	008e\_v3\_s3 & 4.02 & CIVO           & $+$ & $+$ \\
140	008e\_v3\_s2 & 3.78 & IRD (non-CIVO) & $-$ & $-$ \\
141	009b         & 1.62 & transient CIVO & $-$ & $+$ \\
142	001          & 1.00 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
143	008e\_v3\_s4 & 0.99 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
144	008f         & 0.97 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
145	008e\_v3\_s1 & 0.94 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
146	008a         & 0.93 & non-CIVO       & $-$ & $-$ \\
147	\bottomrule
148	\end{tabular}
149	\end{table}
150	
151	\subsection{Multi-Threshold Classification Performance}
152	
153	Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1} reports Task~B1 classification metrics across six NVE thresholds. Task~B1 achieves $\text{AUROC} = 0.917$ because exp\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\times$, self-correcting CIVO, B1-negative) ranks above two sustained-CIVO positives ($4.12\times$, $4.02\times$), breaking the perfect rank separation that held before its inclusion. The optimal threshold lies in the interval $(3.78, 4.02]$; we report $\geq 3.9\times$ as a representative value (accuracy $10/11 = 90.9\%$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2} provides the corresponding analysis for Task~B2 ($\text{AUROC} = 0.967$, imperfect separation due to 008e\_v3\_s2).
154	
155	\begin{table*}[t]
156	\centering
157	\caption{\textbf{Task~B1}: Sustained CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\text{pos}}=3$, $n_{\text{neg}}=8$, $n=11$, $\text{AUROC}=0.917$). All confidence intervals are two-sided Clopper-Pearson exact binomial ($\alpha=0.05$). $^\dagger$Optimal threshold in $(3.78, 4.02]$; exp\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\times$, B1-negative) is the sole false positive.}
158	\label{tab:sensitivity_b1}
159	\small
160	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{5pt}

162	\toprule
163	Threshold & TP & FP & FN & TN & Accuracy [95\% CI] & Precision & Recall & F1 \\
164	\midrule
165	$\geq 1.0\times$ & 3 & 4 & 0 & 4 & 63.6\% \; [30.8, 89.1] & 42.9\% & 100\% & 60.0\% \\
166	$\geq 1.2\times$ & 3 & 3 & 0 & 5 & 72.7\% \; [39.0, 94.0] & 50.0\% & 100\% & 66.7\% \\
167	$\geq 1.5\times$ & 3 & 3 & 0 & 5 & 72.7\% \; [39.0, 94.0] & 50.0\% & 100\% & 66.7\% \\

170	$\geq 2.0\times$ & 3 & 2 & 0 & 6 & 81.8\% \; [48.2, 97.7] & 60.0\% & 100\% & 75.0\% \\
171	\rowcolor{gray!15}
172	$\geq 3.9\times^\dagger$ & 3 & 1 & 0 & 7 & 90.9\% \; [58.7, 99.8] & 75.0\% & 100\% & 85.7\% \\
173	\bottomrule
174	\end{tabular}
175	\end{table*}
176	
177	\begin{table*}[t]
178	\centering
179	\caption{\textbf{Task~B2}: Any CIVO detection sensitivity analysis ($n_{\text{pos}}=5$, $n_{\text{neg}}=6$, $n=11$, $\text{AUROC}=0.967$). The single false positive at $\geq 1.5\times$ is 008e\_v3\_s2 (IRD mechanism, not CIVO). The $1.5\times$ threshold (highlighted) maximizes F1 while maintaining perfect recall.}

218	
219	\subsection{Threshold Recommendation}
220	
221	For the main diagnostic (Task~B2), we recommend $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ as the operating threshold for three reasons:
222	
223	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
224	\item \textbf{Maximum recall.} The $1.5\times$ threshold achieves 100\% recall, detecting all five CIVO cases including the transient case (009b, $\nve = 1.62\times$). Raising the threshold to $1.7\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 80.0\%.
225	\item \textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\times$, F1 = 90.9\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\_v3\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.
226	\item \textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\geq 1.7\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\_v3\_s2 false positive (which requires $\tau > 3.78$).
227	\end{enumerate}
228	
229	For Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), the optimal threshold $\nve \geq 3.9\times$ achieves $10/11$ accuracy ($90.9\%$, 95\% CI: [58.7\%, 99.8\%]); the sole false positive is exp\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\times$, self-correcting CIVO classified as B1-negative).
230	
231	\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}
232	\label{sec:roc_analysis}
233	
234	The original $n{=}17$ analysis (Tables~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}30$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).
235	
236	\begin{figure}[h]
237	\centering
238	\includegraphics[width=0.7\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}
239	\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}30$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.957$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\nve \geq 1.5\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}
240	\label{fig:nve_roc}
241	\end{figure}
242	
243	We label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.
244	
245	The three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\times$) lacks $\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\% of baseline.
246	
247	\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}
248	\label{sec:nve_robustness}
249	
250	To assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).
251	
252	\begin{table}[h]
253	\centering
254	\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}
255	\label{tab:baseline_seeds}
256	\footnotesize
257	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
258	\toprule
259	\textbf{Seed} & \textbf{Eval@105K} & \textbf{Eval@505K} & \textbf{ve@65K} & \textbf{ve@105K} \\
260	\midrule
261	s1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\
262	s2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\
263	s3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\
264	s4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\
265	\bottomrule
266	\end{tabular}
267	\end{table}
268	
269	Seed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\times$) to fall below the $1.5\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.
270	
271	Single-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\%): the boundary case exp\_009b flips from $\nve = 1.62\times$ (s1) to $1.23\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\times \to 4.52\times$; 008d: $4.12\times \to 3.54\times$). The $1.5\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\% of the threshold.
272	
273	\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).
274	
275	\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 11$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.9\% accuracy has 95\% CI [58.7\%, 99.8\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the $n{=}17$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}30$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.957$ (Figure~\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}17$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.972$; the expanded $n{=}30$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.
276	
277	\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}
278	\label{sec:nve_loeo}
279	
280	To assess whether $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}15$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\theta{=}1.5\times$ on the held-out environment.
281	
282	\begin{table}[h]
283	\centering
284	\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\theta=1.5\times$.
285	For each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \emph{not} used
286	to select $\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;
287	$\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}
288	\label{tab:nve_loeo}
289	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
290	\toprule
291	Held-out & $N_{\text{train}}$ & $N_{\text{test}}$ & $\theta_{\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\
292	\midrule
293	PickCube & 20 & 2 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
294	Cheetah  & 17 & 5 & $(1.45,\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\
295	Walker   &  7 & 15 & $(1.00,\, 3.78)$ & 14/15 (0.933) & 0.963 \\
296	\midrule
297	\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \textbf{0.978} & \textbf{0.988} \\
298	\bottomrule
299	\end{tabular}
300	\end{table}
301	
302	The threshold $\theta{=}1.5\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\_008e\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\S\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.978 and mean AUROC is 0.988, confirming that $\theta{=}1.5\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning.
303	PickCube fold confidence intervals remain wide (Clopper-Pearson 95\% CI: $[0.158, 1.000]$) due to $N_{\text{test}}{=}2$.
304

307	\section{Factorial Ablation Details}
308	\label{sec:appendix_factorial}
309	
310	This appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \S\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.
311	
312	\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}
313	Table~\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\dt{=}2$ imagination).
314	
315	\begin{table}[h]
316	\centering
317	\caption{$\dt$-embedding gradient conditions on Walker Walk. Any gradient pathway updating $e_{\dt}$ is sufficient to induce CIVO; freezing or removing the embedding dramatically reduces severity.}
318	\label{tab:three_way_emb}
319	\footnotesize
320	\begin{tabular}{lccccl}
321	\toprule
322	Condition & Grad flow & Exp(s) & NVE peak & Eval & CIVO \\
323	\midrule
324	Trained & all & 008d, 059 & $1.53$--$4.72\times$ & 64--268 & 5/5 \\
325	Reward detach & obs+KL only & 044 & $4.45\times$ & 44$\to$538$^\dagger$ & 1/1 \\
326	Full SGS & KL only & 018 & $1.66\times$ & 448 & 1/1$^*$ \\
327	Frozen & none & 042, 015b, 047, 057 & $0.19$--$1.88\times$$^\ddagger$ & 204--612 & 2/4 \\
328	Removed & N/A & 008f & $0.97$--$1.41\times$ & 307--503 & 0/3 \\
329	\bottomrule
330	\end{tabular}
331	\vspace{1pt}
332	{\raggedright\footnotesize $^*$Full-SGS seed~1 (NVE $1.66\times$); seed~2 collapsed. $^\dagger$Self-corrected at ${\sim}175$K (eval 44$\to$538, 59\% baseline; 224K run). $^\ddagger$Exp\_047 (NVE $1.71\times$) and Exp\_057 (NVE $1.88\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps.\par}
333	\end{table}
334	
335	The reward-detach condition (exp\_044, \texttt{reward\_dt\_emb\_detach=True}) uses a backward hook to block reward-head gradients from reaching $e_{\dt}$, complementing the framework-level Reward SGS (exp\_016e). Both yield the same directional result---CIVO onset not prevented---ruling out implementation artifacts. Exp\_044 exhibits delayed CIVO onset (${\sim}95$K vs.\ typical $25$--$35$K), consistent with reduced but non-zero gradient pressure on $e_{\dt}$. Over a full 224K run, exp\_044 self-corrects: eval recovers from 44 (95K) to 538 (215K, 59\% baseline), but this recovery is incomplete compared to spontaneous recovery without gradient intervention (exp\_045: 868, 95\% baseline). The lower asymptote suggests that selectively disrupting gradient flow may create optimization difficulties beyond CIVO itself.

374	
375	\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}
376	\label{sec:f3}
377	
378	\begin{figure}[t]
379	\centering
380	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}
381	\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}
382	\label{fig:factorial_grid}
383	\end{figure}
384	
385	The empirical finding that trained embedding \emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly predict CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.
386	
387	\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\_008a).}
388	Exp\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.83$--$0.93\times$ range across 3 seeds (105K steps). Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\% of baseline). The critical factor is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.
389	
390	This contrasts with exp\_006 (trained $e_{\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\times$ at 45K to $2.31\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\dt{>}1$ transitions during training. This confirms that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.
391	
392	\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\_015b).}
393	Exp\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \texttt{requires\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.42\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \rightarrow 1.26 \rightarrow 1.45 \rightarrow 0.65 \rightarrow 1.05 \rightarrow 0.47 \rightarrow 0.39 \rightarrow 0.42$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \S\ref{sec:pathways}), the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.
394	
395	The contrast with exp\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \rightarrow 1.71 \rightarrow 2.09 \rightarrow 4.12$) and precedes full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\times$ before self-correcting to $0.42\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\dt$-embedding must be able to \emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.
396	
397	Together, these two controls establish that in all tested configurations, CIVO occurs only with the \emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.
398	
399	Cross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the empirical pattern: trained $e_{\dt}$ precedes CIVO in both tested seeds (exp\_010b, 2 seeds, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\times$, collapse to 51.7/0.83) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). Freezing the embedding on Cheetah (exp\_046, NVE${=}1.35\times$ vs.\ trained $70.64\times$, a $52\times$ reduction) further confirms that embedding trainability, not architecture, drives CIVO. This extends the sufficiency finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\ 2-joint).
400	
401	\begin{table}[ht]
402	\centering
403	\small
404	\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\dt}$ configuration $\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole predictor of CIVO in tested configurations. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}
405	\label{tab:causal_matrix}
406	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
407	\toprule
408	& Multi-rate data (\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\textsc{off}) \\
409	\midrule
410	Trained $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_008d: \textbf{CIVO} & exp\_008a: healthy \\
411	 & NVE 4.12$\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\times$ \\
412	\midrule
413	Frozen $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_015b: grad.\ starvation & --- \\
414	 & peak NVE 1.45$\times$ (transient) & \\
415	\midrule
416	Removed $e_{\dt}$ & exp\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\
417	 & NVE $\leq$0.97$\times$ & \\
418	\bottomrule
419	\end{tabular}
420	\end{table}
421	
422	Only the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) precedes CIVO (Table~\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly sufficient in tested configurations.
423	
424	\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \texorpdfstring{$\dt$}{dt}}
425	\label{sec:f4}
426	
427	\begin{figure}[t]
428	\centering
429	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}
430	\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\%$) $>$ $\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\%$).}
431	\label{fig:dt_amplification}
432	\end{figure}
433	
434	The magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\dt{=}2$ (exp\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\%$), and fixed $\dt{=}4$ (exp\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.
435	
436	\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}
437	\label{sec:f5}
438	
439	Neither horizon length nor temporal subsampling precedes collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\nve < 0.93\times$ (\S\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\nve = 4.12\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\nve > 1.5\times$; low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.
440	
441	The $3.2\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\leq$0.93$\times$).
442	
443	% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---
444	
445	\section{Full Analysis \& Discussion}
446	\label{sec:appendix_analysis}
447	
448	This appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.
449	
450	\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}
451	\label{sec:civo_mechanism}
452	
453	\begin{figure}[t]
454	\centering
455	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}
456	\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\to$ optimistic critic values $\to$ actor exploitation $\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}
457	\label{fig:civo_mechanism}
458	\end{figure}
459	
460	\begin{table}[h]
461	\centering
462	\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\dt{=}1$ vs.\ $\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}
463	\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}
464	\footnotesize
465	\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}
466	\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}
467	\toprule
468	& \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{obs\_MSE}} & \multicolumn{3}{c}{\textbf{reward\_MAE}} \\
469	\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \cmidrule(lr){5-7}
470	\textbf{Config} & $\dt{=}1$ & $\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\dt{=}1$ & $\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\
471	\midrule
472	Baseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\times$ \\
473	008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\times$ \\
474	008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\times$ \\
475	\bottomrule
476	\end{tabular}
477	\end{table}
478	
479	The RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.
480	
481	This asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\nve$ above $1.5\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.
482	
483	A random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\times$ higher mean reward than $\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\approx 36\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\approx 64\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.
484	
485	This mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.
486	
487	\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}
488	To test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \emph{negative} bias at $\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.
489	
490	\begin{table}[h]
491	\centering

493	\label{tab:signed_error}

Raising the threshold to $1.7\\times$ loses 009b, dropping recall to 80.0\\%.\n\\item \\textbf{Best F1.} At $1.5\\times$, F1 = 90.9\\%, the highest among all tested thresholds for B2. The single false positive (008e\\_v3\\_s2) is mechanistically identifiable as IRD rather than CIVO.\n\\item \\textbf{Sensitivity--specificity tradeoff.} Lower thresholds ($1.0\\times$) add false positives without gaining true positives. Higher thresholds ($\\geq 1.7\\times$) sacrifice recall without eliminating the 008e\\_v3\\_s2 false positive (which requires $\\tau > 3.78$).\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nFor Task~B1 (sustained CIVO only), the optimal threshold $\\nve \\geq 3.9\\times$ achieves $10/11$ accuracy ($90.9\\%$, 95\\% CI: [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]); the sole false positive is exp\\_059 (NVE${=}4.72\\times$, self-correcting CIVO classified as B1-negative).\n\n\\subsection{Expanded ROC Analysis}\n\\label{sec:roc_analysis}\n\nThe original $n{=}17$ analysis (Tables~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b1}--\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full}) predates the SGS, RAVE, and TCN experiments. We extend the NVE diagnostic evaluation to $n{=}30$ configurations spanning all tested interventions. Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc} shows the receiver operating characteristic curve with 95\\% bootstrap confidence interval (10{,}000 resamples).\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=0.7\\columnwidth]{../../artifacts/figures/nve_roc_curve.pdf}\n\\caption{NVE ROC curve ($n{=}30$ configurations, AUROC${=}0.957$, in-sample, post-hoc). The preset $\\nve \\geq 1.5\\times$ operating point (star) achieves TPR${=}1.00$, FPR${=}0.18$. Three false positives arise from non-CIVO value overestimation: RAVE anchor pollution, obs-decoder SGS side effects, and IRD eval-driven collapse (008e\\_v3 seed~2). Threshold calibrated and validated in-sample, post-hoc; cross-architecture validation deferred.}\n\\label{fig:nve_roc}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe label a configuration as CIVO-positive if and only if (i)~it uses a trained $\\dt$-embedding with multi-rate data exposure, and (ii)~NVE exceeds $1.5\\times$ with co-occurring entropy collapse. Configurations exhibiting high NVE through other mechanisms (gradient truncation side effects, value anchor pollution) are labeled non-CIVO.\n\nThe three false positives (FPR${=}0.18$) are mechanistically distinct from CIVO: (1)~RAVE anchor pollution (exp\\_028, the EMA anchor becomes inflated, producing overestimation through a different pathway); (2)~obs-decoder SGS-induced value distortion (exp\\_025 seed~2, blocking observation decoder gradients damages the RSSM latent space); (3)~IRD eval-driven collapse (exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2, actor entropy \\emph{increases} with evaluation collapse, mechanistically distinct from CIVO's entropy \\emph{decrease}). All three produce NVE $> 1.5\\times$ through non-CIVO mechanisms, indicating that the NVE diagnostic detects value overestimation broadly rather than CIVO specifically---a feature rather than a limitation for practitioners monitoring deployment health. The closest sub-threshold case (008f seed~4, NVE${=}1.41\\times$) lacks $\\dt$-conditioning entirely ($d{=}0$); its elevated NVE reflects transient value noise that self-corrects without the positive feedback loop characteristic of CIVO. The closest above-threshold positive (008d seed~3, NVE${=}1.53\\times$) exhibits the full CIVO causal chain: trained $\\dt$-embedding, entropy decrease, and evaluation collapse to 14\\% of baseline.\n\n\\subsection{NVE Normalization Robustness}\n\\label{sec:nve_robustness}\n\nTo assess sensitivity to baseline seed choice, we extend the single-seed baseline (seed~1, eval 915.7 at 105K) to three additional seeds trained to 505K steps (Table~\\ref{tab:baseline_seeds}).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Walker Walk baseline seeds at 105K and 505K steps.}\n\\label{tab:baseline_seeds}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Seed} & \\textbf{Eval@105K} & \\textbf{Eval@505K} & \\textbf{ve@65K} & \\textbf{ve@105K} \\\\\n\\midrule\ns1 & 915.7 & 970.4 & 0.563 & 0.656 \\\\\ns2 & 443.5 & 945.7 & 0.898 & 0.742 \\\\\ns3 & 869.0 & 947.7 & --- & 0.664 \\\\\ns4 & 922.8 & 938.9 & --- & 0.624 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nSeed~2 exhibits slow convergence (eval 443.5 at 105K, recovering to 945.7 by 512K), confirming non-pathological slow convergence rather than seed failure. Its elevated $v/e$ ratio at 65K (0.898 vs.\\ 0.563 for seed~1) reflects the low evaluation denominator rather than pathological value overestimation. Using seed~2 as the sole normalization reference systematically deflates NVE values, causing two marginal CIVO-positive configurations (008d seed~3, peak NVE $1.53\\times$; 009b seed~2, peak NVE $1.62\\times$) to fall below the $1.5\\times$ threshold. Core CIVO cases (peak NVE $> 3\\times$) remain correctly classified under all baseline seeds.\n\nSingle-seed normalization (s1) yields AUROC $= 0.933$ and accuracy 7/8 (87.5\\%) on the Walker subset. Four-seed mean normalization yields AUROC $= 0.867$ and accuracy 6/8 (75.0\\%): the boundary case exp\\_009b flips from $\\nve = 1.62\\times$ (s1) to $1.23\\times$ (4-seed mean), because its peak occurs at 45K when the s1 $v/e$ ratio (0.331) is well below the 4-seed mean (0.436). All high-NVE configurations remain correctly classified (006: $4.84\\times \\to 4.52\\times$; 008d: $4.12\\times \\to 3.54\\times$). The $1.5\\times$ threshold thus correctly identifies all strong CIVO cases regardless of baseline choice; sensitivity is confined to configurations within 10\\% of the threshold.\n\n\\paragraph{Normalization conventions.} Walker NVE is normalized against the single-seed baseline (seed~1, exp\\_001, 505K steps). Cheetah NVE is normalized against a 5-seed mean baseline (seeds~0--4, each run to ${\\sim}$210K steps). The differing seed counts reflect available baseline data; both provide sufficient coverage of the respective experimental windows (Walker: 210K analysis window; Cheetah: 105K analysis window).\n\n\\paragraph{Limitations.} With $n = 11$ experiments, Clopper-Pearson confidence intervals remain wide (e.g., 90.9\\% accuracy has 95\\% CI [58.7\\%, 99.8\\%]). These results should be interpreted as a diagnostic proof-of-concept rather than a validated clinical threshold. Replication across additional environments and seeds is needed to establish tighter bounds. Table~\\ref{tab:sensitivity_b2_full} reports the $n{=}17$ evaluation set spanning both environments. The expanded analysis ($n{=}30$, including SGS variants, RAVE, and TCN configurations) yields AUROC${=}0.957$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:nve_roc}, in-sample, post-hoc). The original $n{=}17$ core analysis yields AUROC${=}0.972$; the expanded $n{=}30$ set includes SGS, RAVE, TCN, and dual-critic configurations. Cross-architecture validation is deferred to future work.\n\n\\subsection{Leave-One-Environment-Out Cross-Validation}\n\\label{sec:nve_loeo}\n\nTo assess whether $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes beyond the in-sample evaluation, we perform leave-one-environment-out (LOEO) cross-validation across Walker ($n{=}15$), Cheetah ($n{=}5$), and PickCube ($n{=}2$). For each fold, we hold out all experiments from one environment, select the optimal accuracy-maximizing threshold range on the remaining training set, and evaluate the fixed $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ on the held-out environment.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Leave-one-environment-out cross-validation of NVE threshold $\\theta=1.5\\times$.\nFor each fold, the threshold is validated on an environment whose data was \\emph{not} used\nto select $\\theta$. The optimal threshold range on each training set is reported;\n$\\theta=1.5$ falls within the optimal range in all three folds.}\n\\label{tab:nve_loeo}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nHeld-out & $N_{\\text{train}}$ & $N_{\\text{test}}$ & $\\theta_{\\text{opt}}$ range & Test Accuracy & Test AUROC \\\\\n\\midrule\nPickCube & 20 & 2 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 2/2 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nCheetah  & 17 & 5 & $(1.45,\\, 1.53]$ & 5/5 (1.000) & 1.000 \\\\\nWalker   &  7 & 15 & $(1.00,\\, 3.78)$ & 14/15 (0.933) & 0.963 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{Mean} & — & — & — & \\textbf{0.978} & \\textbf{0.988} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe threshold $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ falls within the optimal accuracy range for all three training folds: $(1.45, 1.53]$ when Walker data is available, and the wider $(1.00, 3.78)$ when training on Cheetah$+$PickCube only (where CIVO and non-CIVO NVE values are well-separated). The single cross-validation error is exp\\_008e\\_v3 seed~2 (Walker), an IRD collapse mechanistically distinct from CIVO (\\S\\ref{sec:roc_analysis}). Mean held-out accuracy is 0.978 and mean AUROC is 0.988, confirming that $\\theta{=}1.5\\times$ generalizes across environments without retuning.\nPickCube fold confidence intervals remain wide (Clopper-Pearson 95\\% CI: $[0.158, 1.000]$) due to $N_{\\text{test}}{=}2$.\n\n% --- APPENDIX B: Factorial Ablation Details ---\n\n\\section{Factorial Ablation Details}\n\\label{sec:appendix_factorial}\n\nThis appendix provides the full factorial ablation results summarized in \\S\\ref{sec:f3f5_summary} of the main text.\n\n\\paragraph{Embedding gradient analysis.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:three_way_emb} isolates embedding \\emph{trainability} as the necessary condition for CIVO by comparing four gradient conditions under otherwise identical configurations ($H{=}8$, subsample ON, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination).\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{$\\dt$-embedding gradient conditions on Walker Walk. Any gradient pathway updating $e_{\\dt}$ is sufficient to induce CIVO; freezing or removing the embedding dramatically reduces severity.}\n\\label{tab:three_way_emb}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccl}\n\\toprule\nCondition & Grad flow & Exp(s) & NVE peak & Eval & CIVO \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained & all & 008d, 059 & $1.53$--$4.72\\times$ & 64--268 & 5/5 \\\\\nReward detach & obs+KL only & 044 & $4.45\\times$ & 44$\\to$538$^\\dagger$ & 1/1 \\\\\nFull SGS & KL only & 018 & $1.66\\times$ & 448 & 1/1$^*$ \\\\\nFrozen & none & 042, 015b, 047, 057 & $0.19$--$1.88\\times$$^\\ddagger$ & 204--612 & 2/4 \\\\\nRemoved & N/A & 008f & $0.97$--$1.41\\times$ & 307--503 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{1pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize $^*$Full-SGS seed~1 (NVE $1.66\\times$); seed~2 collapsed. $^\\dagger$Self-corrected at ${\\sim}175$K (eval 44$\\to$538, 59\\% baseline; 224K run). $^\\ddagger$Exp\\_047 (NVE $1.71\\times$) and Exp\\_057 (NVE $1.88\\times$): both transient, self-correcting within 20K steps.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe reward-detach condition (exp\\_044, \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}) uses a backward hook to block reward-head gradients from reaching $e_{\\dt}$, complementing the framework-level Reward SGS (exp\\_016e). Both yield the same directional result---CIVO onset not prevented---ruling out implementation artifacts. Exp\\_044 exhibits delayed CIVO onset (${\\sim}95$K vs.\\ typical $25$--$35$K), consistent with reduced but non-zero gradient pressure on $e_{\\dt}$. Over a full 224K run, exp\\_044 self-corrects: eval recovers from 44 (95K) to 538 (215K, 59\\% baseline), but this recovery is incomplete compared to spontaneous recovery without gradient intervention (exp\\_045: 868, 95\\% baseline). The lower asymptote suggests that selectively disrupting gradient flow may create optimization difficulties beyond CIVO itself.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:factorial_design} summarizes the experimental configurations.\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Factorial design. Each row specifies one experimental configuration. \\textbf{Emb}: $\\dt$-embedding mode. \\textbf{Sub}: temporal subsampling. \\textbf{H}: imagination horizon. \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$}: temporal resolution used during imagination.}\n\\label{tab:factorial_design}\n\\small\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{3.5pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{ID} & \\textbf{Purpose} & \\textbf{Emb} & \\textbf{Sub} & \\textbf{H} & \\textbf{Imag $\\dt$} \\\\\n\\midrule\n001 & Reference baseline & --- & --- & 15 & 1 \\\\\n006 & Fixed $\\dt{=}2$ & trn & on & 15 & 2 \\\\\n007 & Fixed $\\dt{=}4$ & trn & on & 15 & 4 \\\\\n\\midrule\n008a & Unexposed emb ctrl & exp$^a$ & off & 15 & 2 \\\\\n008c & Horizon control & exp$^a$ & off & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008d & Collapse condition & trn & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n008e\\_v3 & $\\dt{=}1$ imag control & trn & on & 15 & 1 \\\\\n008f & Causal ablation & --- & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n015b & Frozen-emb ctrl & frz$^b$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n016e & Stop-grad (reward) & trn$^\\dagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n018 & Full-SGS & trn$^\\ddagger$ & on & 8 & 2 \\\\\n\\midrule\n005 & Learned gate & adaptive & off & 15 & learned \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\vspace{-2pt}\n{\\raggedright\\footnotesize\ntrn = trained ($d{=}32$, exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data via subsample ON);\nexp$^a$ = trained ($d{=}32$) but never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ data (subsample OFF);\nfrz$^b$ = frozen random projection ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False});\n--- = removed ($d{=}0$).\\\\\n$^\\dagger$ \\texttt{reward\\_dt\\_emb\\_detach=True}; $^\\ddagger$ all non-KL gradients to \\texttt{dt\\_emb} detached.\\par}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{F3: Unexposed Temporal Embedding Suppresses Value Drift}\n\\label{sec:f3}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig3_factorial_grid.pdf}\n\\caption{Factorial isolation of CIVO trigger. Only the combination of trained $e_{\\dt}$ and multi-rate data (upper-left cell) produces pathological NVE. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{fig:factorial_grid}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe empirical finding that trained embedding \\emph{plus} multi-rate exposure jointly predict CIVO rests on two complementary controls that each remove one factor while holding the other constant.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence A: exposure is necessary (exp\\_008a).}\nExp\\_008a uses a trained embedding ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) that is never exposed to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during world-model training (subsample OFF). Despite sharing the same embedding architecture as exp\\_008d, its NVE remains in the $0.83$--$0.93\\times$ range across 3 seeds (105K steps). Performance follows two V-shaped recovery cycles, reaching 777.7 at 105K (85\\% of baseline). The critical factor is not the embedding's capacity to represent temporal information, but its adaptation to multi-rate data that enables precise but inaccurate $\\dt{>}1$ state predictions within the RSSM.\n\nThis contrasts with exp\\_006 (trained $e_{\\dt}$, subsample ON, $H{=}15$), where NVE accelerates from $1.61\\times$ at 45K to $2.31\\times$ at 55K before catastrophic collapse to 132.9 at 65K. Both experiments use identical trained embeddings ($d{=}32$, gradients enabled) and $H{=}15$, differing only in whether the world model sees $\\dt{>}1$ transitions during training. This confirms that multi-rate data exposure, not embedding architecture, is the operative variable.\n\n\\paragraph{Evidence B: trainability is necessary (exp\\_015b).}\nExp\\_015b uses a frozen random-projection embedding ($d{=}32$, \\texttt{requires\\_grad=False}, subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the same multi-rate data regime as exp\\_008d, but with a non-trainable temporal signal. Despite full exposure to $\\dt{>}1$ transitions, the NVE peaks at $1.45\\times$ at 55K but does not sustain escalation: it retreats to $0.65\\times$ by 65K and settles at $0.42\\times$ by 105K (trajectory: $0.81 \\rightarrow 1.26 \\rightarrow 1.45 \\rightarrow 0.65 \\rightarrow 1.05 \\rightarrow 0.47 \\rightarrow 0.39 \\rightarrow 0.42$). The transient peak approaches but does not exceed the $1.5\\times$ CIVO threshold, and critically, the ratio self-corrects rather than exhibiting the monotonic escalation seen in trained-embedding runs. Final evaluation reaches only 204.2 at 105K, low but through a qualitatively different failure mode than CIVO. Actor loss approaches zero (gradient starvation; see \\S\\ref{sec:pathways}), the frozen embedding provides no useful gradient signal for temporal adaptation.\n\nThe contrast with exp\\_008d is stark: under identical data conditions (subsample ON, $H{=}8$), the trained embedding drives NVE to $4.12\\times$ with sustained monotonic escalation ($1.78 \\rightarrow 1.71 \\rightarrow 2.09 \\rightarrow 4.12$) and precedes full collapse (eval 82.8), while the frozen embedding peaks transiently at $1.45\\times$ before self-correcting to $0.42\\times$, a qualitative difference between sustained and transient overestimation. This demonstrates that a $\\dt$-embedding must be able to \\emph{co-adapt} with RSSM dynamics to produce the systematic reward-prediction bias that drives CIVO; a static random projection, regardless of its exposure to multi-rate data, cannot create this bias.\n\nTogether, these two controls establish that in all tested configurations, CIVO occurs only with the \\emph{joint} presence of (i)~a trainable embedding that can co-adapt with RSSM dynamics, and (ii)~multi-rate training data that drives this co-adaptation toward biased temporal predictions. Neither factor alone is sufficient.\n\nCross-environment validation on Cheetah Run replicates the empirical pattern: trained $e_{\\dt}$ precedes CIVO in both tested seeds (exp\\_010b, 2 seeds, NVE $8.27$--$108.52\\times$, collapse to 51.7/0.83) while removing the embedding eliminates it (exp\\_024b, 2 seeds, NVE $<0.2\\times$, eval 96.5/77.3). Freezing the embedding on Cheetah (exp\\_046, NVE${=}1.35\\times$ vs.\\ trained $70.64\\times$, a $52\\times$ reduction) further confirms that embedding trainability, not architecture, drives CIVO. This extends the sufficiency finding from Walker Walk to a second locomotion environment with qualitatively different dynamics (6-joint vs.\\ 2-joint).\n\n\\begin{table}[ht]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Causal isolation: $e_{\\dt}$ configuration $\\times$ multi-rate data exposure. The combination of trained embedding and multi-rate exposure is the sole predictor of CIVO in tested configurations. Neither factor alone is sufficient.}\n\\label{tab:causal_matrix}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& Multi-rate data (\\textsc{on}) & Single-rate data (\\textsc{off}) \\\\\n\\midrule\nTrained $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008d: \\textbf{CIVO} & exp\\_008a: healthy \\\\\n & NVE 4.12$\\times$, collapse & NVE 0.93$\\times$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nFrozen $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_015b: grad.\\ starvation & --- \\\\\n & peak NVE 1.45$\\times$ (transient) & \\\\\n\\midrule\nRemoved $e_{\\dt}$ & exp\\_008f: no CIVO & --- \\\\\n & NVE $\\leq$0.97$\\times$ & \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nOnly the upper-left cell (trained embedding with multi-rate exposure) precedes CIVO (Table~\\ref{tab:causal_matrix}). This 2$\\times$2 structure establishes that both factors are jointly sufficient in tested configurations.\n\n\\subsection{F4: Degradation Scales Monotonically with \\texorpdfstring{$\\dt$}{dt}}\n\\label{sec:f4}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig5_dt_amplification.pdf}\n\\caption{Peak evaluation return as a function of imagination $\\dt$. Performance degrades monotonically: $\\dt{=}1$ (915.7) $>$ $\\dt{=}2$ (485.3, $-47\\%$) $>$ $\\dt{=}4$ (176.9, $-81\\%$).}\n\\label{fig:dt_amplification}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe magnitude of degradation scales monotonically with the imagination step size (Figure~\\ref{fig:dt_amplification}). From the $\\dt{=}1$ baseline (915.7 at 105K), fixed $\\dt{=}2$ (exp\\_006) achieves a peak of only 485.3 ($-47\\%$), and fixed $\\dt{=}4$ (exp\\_007) peaks at 176.9 ($-81\\%$). This monotonic relationship is consistent with compounding model error: larger temporal gaps amplify the RSSM's prediction inaccuracy at each imagination step, and these errors accumulate across the rollout horizon.\n\n\\subsection{F5: Imagination Horizon and Subsampling Are Secondary Factors}\n\\label{sec:f5}\n\nNeither horizon length nor temporal subsampling precedes collapse without multi-rate data exposure: both trained-but-unexposed configurations (008a, 008c) maintain $\\nve < 0.93\\times$ (\\S\\ref{sec:f3}), while the trained-and-exposed counterpart (008d) collapses with $\\nve = 4.12\\times$.  The no-embedding configurations show cross-seed variance (008f: 503.0 vs.\\ 009c: 148.3) but neither exhibits $\\nve > 1.5\\times$; low evaluation alone does not indicate CIVO.\n\nThe $3.2\\times$ performance gap (008a: 777.7 vs.\\ 008c: 240.9) quantifies the horizon effect independently of CIVO, as both conditions maintain healthy NVE ($\\leq$0.93$\\times$).\n\n% --- APPENDIX C: Full Analysis & Discussion ---\n\n\\section{Full Analysis \\& Discussion}\n\\label{sec:appendix_analysis}\n\nThis appendix provides the complete mechanistic analysis summarized in Section~\\ref{sec:discussion} of the main text.\n\n\\subsection{The CIVO Mechanism}\n\\label{sec:civo_mechanism}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig4_mechanism_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{CIVO mechanism diagram. The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables asymmetric reward prediction fidelity, initiating a feedback loop: biased RSSM rewards $\\to$ optimistic critic values $\\to$ actor exploitation $\\to$ reinforced state-distribution skew.}\n\\label{fig:civo_mechanism}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{RSSM fidelity at $\\dt{=}1$ vs.\\ $\\dt{=}2$. Trained embedding (008d) produces $2.78\\times$ reward gap; removing it (008f) eliminates the gap but degrades absolute prediction.}\n\\label{tab:rssm_fidelity}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\n& \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{obs\\_MSE}} & \\multicolumn{3}{c}{\\textbf{reward\\_MAE}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-7}\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap & $\\dt{=}1$ & $\\dt{=}2$ & Gap \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 13.49 & 27.50 & 2.04$\\times$ & 0.0102 & 0.0159 & 1.56$\\times$ \\\\\n008d (trained) & 41.31 & 53.67 & 1.30$\\times$ & 0.0568 & 0.1579 & 2.78$\\times$ \\\\\n008f (no emb) & 8.01 & 22.04 & 2.75$\\times$ & 0.7469 & 0.7526 & 1.01$\\times$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nThe RSSM fidelity analysis (Table~\\ref{tab:rssm_fidelity}) reveals the mechanistic core of CIVO (Figure~\\ref{fig:civo_mechanism}).  The trained $\\dt$-embedding enables the reward head to learn temporally differentiated predictions, but these predictions are asymmetrically accurate: reward MAE at $\\dt{=}1$ is $0.0568$, while at $\\dt{=}2$ it rises to $0.1579$, a $2.78\\times$ gap.  This gap is not an artifact of prediction difficulty alone; the no-embedding configuration (008f) exhibits a $1.01\\times$ gap, and the baseline shows $1.56\\times$.  The trained embedding actively amplifies the fidelity discrepancy by allowing the reward head to co-adapt with the temporal signal.\n\nThis asymmetric fidelity initiates a feedback loop with four stages: (1) the trained $\\dt$-embedding causes RSSM predictions at $\\dt > 1$ to carry systematically biased reward estimates; (2) the critic, trained entirely on imagined trajectories, internalizes these biased rewards as optimistic value estimates; (3) the actor exploits the overestimated regions of the value landscape, shifting its policy toward states that appear high-value only under the biased model; and (4) this policy shift further skews the distribution of imagined states, reinforcing the bias.  The diagnostic signature is a sustained $\\nve$ above $1.5\\times$, followed by evaluation collapse.\n\nA random-policy control experiment ($N{=}100$ rollouts, 95\\% bootstrap CI) decomposes CIVO into two necessary stages.  \\textbf{Stage~1 (dynamics bias):} even under a random policy, $\\dt{=}2$ imagination produces $1.68\\times$ higher mean reward than $\\dt{=}1$ in the trained-embedding model (008d): the RSSM prior already generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$, independent of actor behavior.  \\textbf{Stage~2 (actor exploitation):} the trained actor learns to exploit these biased regions, amplifying the ratio to $2.90\\times$.  In the no-embedding control (008f), neither stage is present: the random-policy ratio is $1.11\\times$ and the trained-actor ratio is $0.98\\times$.  The decomposition attributes $\\approx 36\\%$ of the total effect to dynamics bias and $\\approx 64\\%$ to actor exploitation, which confirms that both stages are necessary for CIVO to manifest.\n\nThis mechanism is analogous to delusional bias \\citep{lu2018nondelusional}, but differs in origin: delusional bias arises from policy-class constraints, while CIVO is model-induced.  The consequence (policy exploitation of overestimated regions) is similar, but the causal pathway is distinct.\n\n\\paragraph{Signed reward error analysis.}\nTo test whether CIVO arises from one-step reward overprediction, we compute signed error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions (Table~\\ref{tab:signed_error}). The trained-embedding model (008d) shows \\emph{negative} bias at $\\dt{=}2$ (mean signed error $-0.599$, only 8\\% of samples overpredicted), ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the mechanism. The baseline is well-calibrated (mean SE $\\approx 0$), confirming methodological soundness. Value overestimation therefore occurs during \\emph{imagination-level} prior drift: the RSSM prior (unconditioned on observations) generates reward-favorable trajectories at $\\dt > 1$ (Stage~1 above, $1.68\\times$ ratio under random policy), and the critic accumulates these into overestimated returns. The one-step reward prediction is negatively biased when conditioned on real observations, but this posterior correction is unavailable during imagination. This also distinguishes CIVO from generic compounding error, which would produce \\emph{positive} signed error accumulating with horizon; the negative observation-level bias confirms that CIVO is a prior-drift phenomenon, not a reward-prediction artifact.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Signed reward error (predicted $-$ actual) on posterior-conditioned RSSM predictions. Negative values indicate underprediction. The trained-embedding model underpredicts at $\\dt{=}2$, ruling out observation-level reward overprediction as the CIVO mechanism.}\n\\label{tab:signed_error}\n\\footnotesize\n\\setlength{\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Config} & $\\dt$ & \\textbf{Mean SE} & \\textbf{Std} & \\textbf{\\%Over} & \\textbf{Actual} & \\textbf{Pred} \\\\\n\\midrule\nBaseline & 1 & $+0.001$ & 0.023 & 39.6\\% & 1.893 & 1.894 \\\\",
      "numLines": 500,
